### [Why Even Experts Don’t Know What to Do About AI Risk](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tRRkj249gdDL4mued/why-even-experts-don-t-know-what-to-do-about-ai-risk)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 76.72536385173908%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780413025/lexical_client_uploads/xfwdw0kyzw13fug1llqh.png" /></figure><p><span>AI Safety veteran Holden Karnofsky thinks there’s a 49% chance his actions are making things worse.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbxyprx3nt95"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbxyprx3nt95">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>In 2025, Jesse Clifton even </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/YE3tdpE6JdiWRqqKx/leadership-change-at-the-center-on-long-term-risk" rel="noreferrer"><span>stepped down</span></a><span> as the executive director of the Center on Long-Term risk because of similar reasons.</span></p><p><span>Even top AI Safety strategists don’t know what will make things better, and what will make things worse.</span></p><p><span>Why is it so hard to improve humanity’s odds?</span></p><p><span>And what can you do to choose your actions?</span></p><h2><span>1) Hidden Failure Lets You Fail Without Knowing It</span></h2><p><span>In AI Safety, impact is hard to measure, and thus lack of impact is often invisible. We call this "hidden failure".  With hidden failure, projects fail to have a positive impact but the people doing the project don’t realise it.</span></p><p><span>To understand where hidden failure comes from, it’s useful to understand reasons why projects fail in general. These reasons fall on a spectrum:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Wrong problem:</span></b><span> You're addressing something with little influence on x-risk. For example, researching AI fairness when the core risk is misalignment.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Wrong solution:</span></b><span> Your solution doesn't solve the problem, even when competently executed. E.g. interpretability research that's technically novel but isn’t actually helpful.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Poor execution:</span></b><span> Your problem-solution set could be impactful but you're not executing your solution competently enough.</span></li></ul><p><span>These factors can cause problems with both of the things you need to be impactful – </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jDDZsoGRK8HNZEoTb/two-skillsets-you-need-to-launch-an-impactful-ai-safety" rel="noreferrer"><span>adoption and effectiveness</span></a><span>:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>A </span><b><span>lack of adoption</span></b><span> is relatively easy to spot if you want to</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvylu4jdy0s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvylu4jdy0s">[2]</a></sup></span><span> and can be remedied by </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xaQMada2Cpo98hM4E/startup-lessons-for-ai-safety" rel="noreferrer"><span>entrepreneurial iteration</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><span>A </span><b><span>lack of impact-effectiveness</span></b><span>,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdgldixeldje"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndgldixeldje">[3]</a></sup></span><span> in contrast, can be particularly hard to spot, and that’s what we’re calling </span><b><span>“hidden failure”</span></b><span> in this post.</span></li></ul><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 79.41122290473619%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/2c0ca20dffb7398d27e13b962c41e9cb9134779b6c7682372d3b9d1cb22a7ec2/hfnhpvu0gbznjqujswa3" /></figure><p><span>With hidden failure, you might have users, citations, and funding (i.e. you have “adoption”), and still fail to have impact or even make things worse.</span></p><p><span>Let us put that more bluntly: </span><b><span>It’s literally possible for all your friends to think you’re successful and still be making things worse</span></b><span>. Even within AI Safety. Even outside of frontier labs.</span></p><h2><span>2) Why impact is harder than profit</span></h2><p><span>Creating a profitable startup is hard. Achieving impact in AI Safety is even harder for several reasons:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>There is no clear (market) signal to guide you. In other words, it’s hard to measure success.</span></li><li value="2"><span>To have impact, you need both adoption (like a for-profit)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwynp8mvyjwr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwynp8mvyjwr">[4]</a></sup></span><span> AND effectiveness (unlike a standard for-profit).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefn3r1eontqgi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnn3r1eontqgi">[5]</a></sup></span><span> In many ways, impact doesn’t just pose </span><i><span>different </span></i><span>challenges than profit. It poses </span><i><span>extra</span></i><span> challenges.</span></li><li value="3"><span>AI Safety is largely pre-paradigmatic.</span></li></ul><h2><span>3) The pre-paradigmatic challenge</span></h2><p><span>AI Safety doesn't have an established paradigm yet.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5ty4rn5gd78"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5ty4rn5gd78">[6]</a></sup></span><span> We can't predict with certainty what will be impactful. So why bother optimizing so deliberately?</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 83.87514641104846%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6b95ef5b48cd3e00fa4406f78f5026853cf7481d36b681b0ce5ac83a75d22cfe/yp05dgkmi6mw0bia0muy" /></figure><p><span>First, imperfect predictions are still valuable. For example, AI Safety experts can often point out specific reasons why a given project or idea is unlikely to be impactful.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6p77ann9q9s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6p77ann9q9s">[7]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Secondly, we argue the lack of a paradigm actually makes deliberate thinking about impact </span><i><span>more</span></i><span> important, not </span><i><span>less</span></i><span>. Without clear guides on what will lead to impact, you have to figure it out yourself.</span></p><p><span>The tools described in the next posts help you optimize for impact under uncertainty. The goal isn't to get it perfectly right or to cripple yourself with analysis paralysis.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6oz6h48cze5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6oz6h48cze5">[8]</a></sup></span><span> But we do think most people would benefit from spending more time thinking about their impact.</span></p><p><span>So let's think strategically about impact. We’ll give a high-level overview of how to do that in an upcoming post, and we’ll help you measure your impact in another one.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbxyprx3nt95"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbxyprx3nt95">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We’re paraphrasing that from </span><a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/holden-karnofsky-concrete-ai-safety-frontier-ai-companies" rel="noreferrer"><span>his appearance on the 80,000 hours podcast</span></a><span>, around the 4:11:30 mark, where he said: “I think overall I would probably agree with you that the smaller you’re making the scope of where you’re hoping to have impact, the more reasonable it is to be like 60/40. But most people who go into AI are not going into it for that. Otherwise, if you want a small-scope, robustly positive impact, you should maybe work in a cause like farm animal welfare or global poverty. For the size of impact that tends to motivate people, I think it does get partially offset by this huge uncertainty about the sign.</span></p><p><span>I tend to think it’s worse than 51/49. I tend to think we’re always going to be prone to overestimate how robustly good our actions are. And the more we learn about all the galaxy-brained considerations that one should have had in one’s head, the more it’s going to be like 50+ε%. I think AI safety is a great cause to work in. I’m excited to work in it. I think it’s high impact. I am doing my best to do things that I will be proud to have done and hope for the best. But I really do have to live with the possibility that my ultimate impact on the utilons or whatever is going to be negative.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvylu4jdy0s"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvylu4jdy0s">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Though you shouldn’t underestimate your brain’s ability to make itself comfortable, satisfice, and employ motivated reasoning to have you accept mediocrity.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndgldixeldje"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdgldixeldje">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We’re using “impact-effectiveness” as a synonym for “effectiveness” as meant by the Impact Equation: Impact = Adoption x Effectiveness.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnwynp8mvyjwr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefwynp8mvyjwr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I will refer here and in other place to for-profits as regular companies not aimed at AI Safety. Of course, an AI Safety project can be set up as a for-profit too.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnn3r1eontqgi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefn3r1eontqgi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Although arguably, adoption is </span><i><span>sometimes</span></i><span> easier in a nonprofit setting. For example, the various fellowships have no trouble finding enough participants. In contrast, though, many products, tools, and blog posts do struggle to get adoption.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5ty4rn5gd78"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5ty4rn5gd78">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See e.g. </span><a href="https://ai-safety-atlas.com/chapters/03/07"><span>https://ai-safety-atlas.com/chapters/03/07</span></a><span> or </span><a href="https://www.thecompendium.ai/ai-safety"><span>https://www.thecompendium.ai/ai-safety</span></a><span>. Although instead of saying AI Safety is pre-paradigmatic, it’s more accurate to say that none of the existing paradigms is widely agreed to be sufficient for making the world safe, especially by higher level researchers in that paradigm. Aka, we have a bunch of paradigms, but they’re all pretty limited, and all-in-all we don’t even know yet what approaches will be required to make the world safe enough.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6p77ann9q9s"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6p77ann9q9s">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Though there are also areas where experts disagree. In such cases, it becomes even more important to assess the specific arguments they use.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6oz6h48cze5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6oz6h48cze5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See e.g. </span><a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/holden-karnofsky-concrete-ai-safety-frontier-ai-companies/#transcript"><span>Holden Karnofsky on the 80000 hours podcast</span></a><span>, where he says "When people ask me for career advice or whatever, the usual thing I’d say is: take a bunch of options that all seem competitive, and all seem like they could be the best thing, and that it’s not obvious which ones are better than others from an impact perspective. And from there I would say go with personal fit, go with the energy you feel to work on them."</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tRRkj249gdDL4mued/why-even-experts-don-t-know-what-to-do-about-ai-risk#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Announcing the ARC White-Box Estimation Challenge](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Kben8CzS4awCwNw5c/announcing-the-arc-white-box-estimation-challenge)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>ARC has teamed up with <a href="https://www.aicrowd.com/">AIcrowd</a> to launch the <a href="https://www.aicrowd.com/challenges/white-box-estimation-challenge-2026"><strong>ARC White-Box Estimation Challenge</strong></a>, a contest to improve upon our estimation algorithms for <a href="https://www.alignment.org/blog/mechanistic-estimation-for-wide-random-mlps/">random MLPs</a>. The warm-up round begins this week, and later rounds will have a total prize pool of at least $100,000.</p>
<p>We are very grateful to Sharada Mohanty, Sneha Nanavati, Dipam Chakraborty and everyone else at AIcrowd for working with us to host this contest, as well as to Paul Rosu for testing the contest and to Harshita Khera for operational support.</p>
<h2>Introduction to the Challenge</h2>
<p>Our challenge follows the same setup as our <a href="https://www.alignment.org/blog/mechanistic-estimation-for-wide-random-mlps/">recent paper</a> on wide random MLPs: we consider MLPs  with weights , defined by<br />
<br />
where the activation function  is , applied coordinatewise.</p>
<p><img alt="mlp_arch.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/fsG4m6sRMpomd7Rk6/szypoqi5ljlo7cpqdic8" /><br />
</p><p>To begin with, we are fixing the width  and the number of hidden layers , but we expect to change this setup in future rounds.<sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1">[1]</a></sup></p>
<p>Contestants must design an algorithm that takes in a set of weights  and produces an estimate for the expected output<br />
</p>
<p>Algorithms will be evaluated on MLPs with randomly-sampled Gaussian weights. The goal is to achieve as low mean squared error as possible, subject to certain computational constraints.</p>
<p>We have devised a <a href="https://github.com/AIcrowd/flopscope">FLOP-counting scheme</a> with AIcrowd to minimize any advantage from using heavily optimized numerical kernels, allowing participants to focus on higher-level algorithm design instead. This scheme may still have a few rough edges remaining, but we hope to round these out over the course of the warm-up round.</p>
<p>For further details, please see the <a href="https://www.aicrowd.com/challenges/white-box-estimation-challenge-2026">challenge website</a>.</p>
<h2>Why run this contest?</h2>
<p>In the long run, we would like to answer questions about highly intelligent AI systems such as, "Are there unusual situations in which the system would undermine human control?". Running the system on a huge number of different inputs may not be a reliable way to answer such questions, since a highly intelligent system may not fall for our "honey pots". This why we are interested in white-box approaches that leverage our access to the model's internals. Ultimately, of course, we should use whichever methods perform the best.</p>
<p>Unfortunately, designing highly performant white-box estimation methods for trained networks is challenging, even for <a href="https://www.alignment.org/blog/algzoo-uninterpreted-models-with-fewer-than-1-500-parameters/">tiny models</a>. ARC's bet is that we can build up to this challenge by first producing performant white-box estimation methods for randomly-initialized networks, and then figuring out how those methods can be adapted with each step of training. However, even this first step remains incomplete. In our <a href="https://www.alignment.org/blog/mechanistic-estimation-for-wide-random-mlps/">recent paper</a>, we produced white-box methods that outperform black-box methods for MLPs with large width, but they break down as the depth grows, and we are very confident that our methods can be significantly improved.</p>
<p>By running this contest, we hope to spur others to discover such improvements. Even though "white-box" is in the name of the challenge, contestants are permitted to use any methods they choose, whether white-box or black-box: as stated above, we ultimately want the best-performing algorithm. However, we strongly expect the best possible algorithms for this problem to be "mechanistic" (i.e., to avoid black-box sampling entirely), mirroring our existing results in the large width setting.</p>
<h2>Use of LLMs</h2>
<p>We encourage contestants to use LLMs to whatever extent helps them improve their submissions the most. In later rounds, there will be two kinds of prize: one for the best-performing submission, and one for our favorite algorithmic contribution described in a technical report. Especially for the latter kind of prize, contestants may benefit from having a good understanding of any LLM-written code themselves, but the rules of the contest do not require this.</p>
<p>In fact, exploring LLM usage is another motivation for holding the contest. Thanks to how our research has developed, it now looks possible to make progress on some of our core problems by hill-climbing on well-defined metrics, which is exciting to us.<sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2">[2]</a></sup> At the same time, the ability of LLMs to make considerable progress on such problems is improving rapidly, and we want to position ourselves to take full advantage of this. We are not sure whether we will be able to draw generalizable insights from strong submissions that are primarily LLM-written, but we think putting LLMs to work on the problem is a worthwhile experiment nonetheless.</p>
<p>As a word of caution, our FLOP-counting utility is definitely hackable in ways that would be very unambiguously hacking once pointed out, such as by modifying constants or counts held in memory. Contestants are responsible for ensuring that their submissions do not hack our FLOP-counting utility, regardless of whether or how they choose to use LLMs.</p>
<p>To all contestants: good luck!</p>
<hr />
<section class="footnotes">
<ol>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn1"><p>The contest setup is actually slightly different in that it omits the final linear layer, but this makes essentially no difference. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn2"><p>We have previously offered prizes for solutions to problems, but they have either been <a href="https://www.alignment.org/blog/prizes-for-elk-proposals/">more pedagogical</a> or <a href="https://www.alignment.org/blog/prize-for-matrix-completion-problems/">less central</a> to our agenda. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
</section><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Kben8CzS4awCwNw5c/announcing-the-arc-white-box-estimation-challenge#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Agent Foundations Reminds Me of Continental Philosophy](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GykcHo2rfex3YHPuD/agent-foundations-reminds-me-of-continental-philosophy)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <blockquote><p><span>Nevertheless, I shall take advantage of your kindness in assuming we agree that a science cannot be conditioned upon empiricism.</span></p><p><span>— Jacques Lacan, “The Subversion of the Subject and the Dialectic of Desire in the Freudian Unconscious”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcc70tbvj3ev"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncc70tbvj3ev">[1]</a></sup></span></p></blockquote><p><span>Freud developed the first modern theory of the unconscious. His writings on drives, dreams and the id were instrumental in developing modern practices of psychology and neuroscience. Modern researchers are unlikely to leverage concepts like the superego or the oedipal complex, because we have been able to further our understanding of the mind through empirical work, which does not support many of Freud’s specific claims. Freud pushed us in the right direction, but he lacked an empirical foundation to make precise claims.</span></p><p><span>Freudian psychoanalysis mapped well to our narrative claims about the mind. Particularly coming out of the prudishness of late 19th century Europe, it was intuitive to learn we all had unconscious drives that did not track with societal norms. Stefan Zweig, writing about the austere Austro-Hungarian norms of his youth, wrote:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>The more a woman was supposed to appear as a “lady,” the less her natural forms were permitted to be discernible; fundamentally, fashion, with this deliberate guiding principle of hers, was merely serving obediently the general moral tendency of the time, whose chief concern was covering and concealing.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgg6vsboc58"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngg6vsboc58">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>— Stefan Zweig, </span><i><span>Die Welt von Gestern</span></i><span> (1942)</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Leaving this period of social conservatism for the competing liberalisation and violence of the first half of the 20th century, it should not be surprising if the public was eager to learn about our suppressed tendencies, nor if they continued to explore this narrative framework far past its utility.</span></p><p><span>In 1952, Jacques Lacan began a series of seminars on his idiosyncratic psychoanalytic theory, which would later be published as “Écrits” in 1966. Building on the work of Freud and connecting it with the structuralist linguistics developed by Ferdinand de Saussure, Lacan’s work is famously dense. He builds a new vocabulary to situate the self beside language and sexual desire/anxiety, introducing terms like big Other and little other with distinct symbols (A, a) and “algebraic” relationships. This leads to graphs like</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 47.129909365558916%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/GykcHo2rfex3YHPuD/bjxgjclogx31zqb12ts1" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Lacan, Écrits (Seuil, 1966), 817</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>and equations like</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 83.08157099697885%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/GykcHo2rfex3YHPuD/hyldxdafxguqvmigojw5" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Lacan, Écrits (Seuil, 1966), 819</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Both examples come from “The Subversion of the Subject and the Dialectic of Desire,” which is generally understood to be one of the best introductory texts on Lacan.</span></p><p><span>Recall that, by the time of the publication of “Écrits,” the “</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_revolution" rel="noreferrer"><span>cognitive revolution</span></a><span>” had been underway for nearly a decade. This would eventually mature into cognitive science, and our modern understanding of psychology, neuroscience, linguistics and artificial intelligence.</span></p><p><span>Understanding Lacan’s work presents a unique intellectual challenge for his readers. Working through his formalizations can be fun, in the same way it is exciting to figure out a complicated puzzle. The use of symbols and algebraic notation provides the allure of sophistication that prose alone cannot. Making statements like “castration represents the disavowal of the Real by the Imaginary” is a helpful signal of one’s own intellect.</span></p><p><span>Theory does not require strictly empirical work, but building theories on top of theories creates castles in the sky, with little predictive power for reality.</span></p><p><span>In college, I spent a a lot of time studying the French intellectual tradition. Not everyone in the tradition is so separated from reality. Writers like Albert Camus articulated a meaningful engagement with life in a post-theistic society; intellectuals like Foucault turned to history to reveal our latent power structures. But obscurantist authors like Lacan and Derrida have spawned numerous imitators. As a former Arrogant Student, I can attest that writing in reference to this dense terminology can make one feel satisfyingly smart. But I think the primary motivation behind authors in this tradition has been to drape their writing in a pretense of rigor, stealing the formalisms of science to write without its methodologies.</span></p><p><span>Theory, to remain rigorous, needs to conceptualize and assess its own ties to reality. If you remain too bound to present understanding, you will never advance knowledge. But if you build upon layers of abstraction, without careful justification, you will deviate from an accurate model of ground truth.</span></p><hr /><p><span>As I have begun to research superintelligence alignment in the past year, particularly agent foundations, I have sometimes had the uncanny feeling that I was back in college studying continental philosophy. Reading about functional decision theory and deep deceptiveness certainly makes me </span><i><span>feel</span></i><span> like I am learning about the challenges of alignment. But much of the material in the field has been written in such a way that it is difficult to unpack the chain of assumptions leading to a single argument. Rather than dealing with crisp theoretical formulations which make explicit claims, one argues through a series of metaphors, with an ill-defined link back to reality.</span></p><p><span>I believe this is partly due to the specific writing style of Eliezer Yudkowsky, which tends to be </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SA9hDewwsYgnuscae/projectlawful-com-eliezer-s-latest-story-past-1m-words" rel="noreferrer"><span>verbose</span></a><span> and prone to metaphor and other narrative approaches of argumentation. As a foundational figure in alignment research, it makes sense that he would continue to hold sway over present styles of authorship. But if Yudkowsky’s style is well-suited for introducing an online, technical audience to alignment problems, it is poorly suited for arguing those claims robustly against specific, material considerations.</span></p><p><span>A lot of commentary on AI risk and alignment theory takes place on LessWrong. As a community blog intended to provoke new directions of thought, LessWrong is fantastic! An important first step in developing new theories is to work on concepts, while formalization and evidence come later. But a lot of actual alignment work also occurs directly on LessWrong, or the associated AI Alignment Forum. Because of this dual use, I feel that there has been a mode collapse, where it is difficult to distinguish between metaphorical/conceptual work and rigorous research.</span></p><p><span>Consider this example from Andrew Critch’s </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/LWJsgNYE8wzv49yEc" rel="noreferrer"><span>boundaries sequence</span></a><span> (which I liked a lot but was fresh on my mind). Critch introduces a cross-disciplinary concept of “boundaries,” which he suggests are an important precept in modeling agent behavior and preferences. Early in the sequence, he uses the example of an employee who can/cannot maintain a good boundary between their personal life and work.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 81.72205438066466%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/GykcHo2rfex3YHPuD/pkbo81pgbqsnorcorxog" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Critch, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/vnJ5grhqhBmPTQCQh/boundaries-part-2-trends-in-ea-s-handling-of-boundaries"><span>«Boundaries» Part 2</span></a><span> (2022)</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>This is a nice conceptual framing! It does seem that “boundaries” are a useful emergent property of a number of different theories, which merits further thinking. I don’t have a good formal conception of what boundaries might look like, but I can intuit how they will behave in practice.</span></p><p><span>Later in the sequence, Critch attempts to formalize boundaries using an “approximate directed (dynamic) Markov blanket.”</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 64.19939577039275%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/GykcHo2rfex3YHPuD/gpyqwaw93ywyretgxc5b" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Critch, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HrtqLy46Fx7xqRrMo/boundaries-part-3a-defining-boundaries-as-directed-markov"><span>«Boundaries» Part 3a</span></a><span> (2022)</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Critch’s formalization does not come out of thin air. Active inference, for example, also uses Markov blankets to formalize the boundary of a single agent. But I worry that we have now </span><i><span>overformalized</span></i><span> a conceptual theory, or attempted to turn metaphor into math! While formalization does allow for greater specificity and may feel intuitive to individuals coming from a background heavy in math, I worry that this brings an unjustified level of precision to a solution that is inherently approximate.</span></p><p><span>Does this bring us closer to a transferable understanding of boundaries? Or have we allowed ourselves to get lost in unnecessary details?</span></p><p><span>Far more problematic to me are arguments like </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3pinFH3jerMzAvmza/on-how-various-plans-miss-the-hard-bits-of-the-alignment" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>the sharp left turn</span></a><span>, which become load-bearing in some misalignment scenarios, but are poorly specified in the work of Nate Soares and others. As Paul Christiano astutely comments:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>More concretely, you talk about novel mechanisms by which AI systems gain capabilities, but </span><b><span>I think you haven’t said much concrete about why existing alignment work couldn’t address these mechanisms</span></b><span>. This looks to me like a pretty unproductive stance; I suspect you are wrong about the shape of the problem, but if you are right then I think your main realistic path to impact involves saying something more concrete about why you think this.</span></p><p><span>— Paul Christiano, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3pinFH3jerMzAvmza/on-how-various-plans-miss-the-hard-bits-of-the-alignment?commentId=qdkKysrRTs8joWRsf"><span>commenting on Soares’s post</span></a><span>; emphasis my own</span></p></blockquote><p><span>I worry that some alignment researchers have become averse to empiricism and rigorous justification of claims. LessWrong provides a low-stakes environment to share sketches of ideas that will be formalized elsewhere. But some never seem to escape the stylistic pull of the internet blog format. Mixing informal and formal techniques creates mimetic risk for the alignment researcher.</span></p><p><span>Writers are usually upfront when their thoughts are cursory or speculative. The “epistemic status” label is meant to be an honest acknowledgement that different posts come with different strengths of claims. But in practice, the label functions as a permission slip to say whatever one wants. As long as you’re honest that your claims are groundless, then there’s nothing to stop you from making your claims! As Slavoj Zizek put it in a defense of psychoanalysis:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Isn’t my virtual persona in a way ‘more real than reality’? Isn’t it precisely because I am aware that this is ‘just a game’ that in it I can do what I would never be able to in the real world?</span></p><p><span>— Slavoj Zizek, “</span><a href="https://www.lrb.co.uk/the-paper/v28/n10/slavoj-zizek/freud-lives" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Freud lives!</span></a><span>” (2006)</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Can’t writing on LessWrong also feel like a game, where the objective is to cleverly restate your idea with mathematics or tie it back to a niche concept from The Sequences?</span></p><p><span>There is a lot of good discourse in the community. I do not mean to devalue how important some of the problems raised by agent foundations can be. But we should be careful not to reside </span><i><span>too</span></i><span> long in the “virtual” world of speculative discourse, which leads to play and clever anecdotes, rather than scientific advancement and technological change.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fncc70tbvj3ev"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcc70tbvj3ev">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Écrits, trans. Bruce Fink (Norton, 2006), 672</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngg6vsboc58"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgg6vsboc58">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Translation by Claude</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GykcHo2rfex3YHPuD/agent-foundations-reminds-me-of-continental-philosophy#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Tech I'm skeptical of and why](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Y5cQYKYwAb2WwXXQQ/tech-i-m-skeptical-of-and-why)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>I’m a fan of people trying things, even if they seem silly. Dismissing risky ideas misses the point of research.</span></p><p><span>But thoughtful criticism can direct effort to more promising fields. To that end, I’m going to try to make my criticism as constructive as possible, with concrete reasons for my pessimism and closely related research areas which are promising (and stand to benefit even from failures in the field I’m criticizing).</span></p><p><span>Not every section will live up to that standard; some arguments are built on vibes alone. I might be wrong, so I still want to see people work on these problems.</span></p><h1><span>Preliminaries</span></h1><h3><span>Exhaustible resources more expensive long-term</span></h3><p><span>The </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotelling's_rule"><span>theory</span></a><span> of exhaustible resources </span><a href="https://www.economicforces.xyz/p/how-should-we-think-about-the-strategic"><span>suggests</span></a><span> that “the real price of an exhaustible resource should grow at a rate equal to the real interest rate.” The data </span><a href="https://open.substack.com/pub/splittinginfinity/p/links-31?r=f8fjw&amp;selection=1a603e7c-1db2-4aa6-8b5a-e6d1933116d8"><span>are consistent</span></a><span> with this hypothesis</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8ua367vjn0u"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8ua367vjn0u">[1]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Innovations in search and extraction can dramatically lower prices in the short term, but in the long term, we converge on a </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/p/breakthroughs-rare-and-decreasing"><span>correct way to do things</span></a><span>. Once extraction is solved, prices return to their slow upward climb.</span></p><h3><span>Idiot index</span></h3><p><span>Raw materials are just a fraction of the final cost of a good. You need to take out loans to buy the materials. You need land and trucks. You need to buy equipment. You need people to design, build, and test.</span></p><p><span>(And robots do not solve labor costs. Robots are made of raw materials and equipment and loans. Most importantly, like humans, robots have an opportunity cost, other uses of the robot bid up the price. Robots will have a non-zero “wage”.)</span></p><p><span>It’s usually straightforward to estimate input costs, but these other costs (land, equipment, wages, etc.) are tricky. I’ll try to get around this with the </span><a href="https://substack.exponentialindustry.com/p/ratio-of-finished-cost-to-material-cost-musk"><span>idiot index</span></a><span>: the ratio of the final cost of a good to the cost of the raw materials. An example for metals:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tgHpawHG4NbGhfghv/arjz02qyci70dkxkfsm9" /><p><a href="https://www.orcasciences.com/articles/checking-my-prejudices-on-materials-decarbonization"><span>Source</span></a></p><p><span>We’ll use the idiot index as a heuristic to think about the hard-to-estimate costs of producing a good. If we know the raw materials cost and an idiot index for similar products, we can guesstimate the final cost. Simpler processes have a lower idiot index while complex processes a higher one.</span></p><p><span>But don’t take this too seriously, the index I use for different areas is just an educated guess. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9Tw5RqnEzqEtaoEkq/if-it-s-worth-doing-it-s-worth-doing-with-made-up-statistics"><span>If It’s Worth Doing, It’s Worth Doing With Made-Up Statistics</span></a><span>.</span></p><h3><span>BOTEC’s are inherently optimistic</span></h3><p><span>Armchair estimation of costs is inherently optimistic. </span><a href="http://johnsalvatier.org/blog/2017/reality-has-a-surprising-amount-of-detail"><span>Reality has a surprising amount of detail</span></a><span>, you often miss major cost contributors until you actually start doing the thing.</span></p><p><span>This is a feature, I’ll be employing an </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/p/optimistic-cost-benefit-analysis"><span>optimistic cost benefit analysis</span></a><span> for the technologies I’m criticizing. I want to make the best possible case before I rule out an idea. But don’t be fooled, even if something looks slightly better on paper, complicated plans often fail in practice.</span></p><p><span>Preliminaries aside, let’s start critiquing.</span></p><h1><span>e-Fuels</span></h1><p><span>Producing fuel from CO2 capture and then burning it again </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/i/166474350/load-bearing-assumptions"><span>doesn’t make sense</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>… e-fuels and sustainable aviation fuel are silly. Capturing carbon *and *making fuel is probably more expensive than just capturing carbon. Making e-fuels requires capturing carbon </span><i><span>plus</span></i><span> making green hydrogen *plus *using renewable energy *plus *building reactors </span><i><span>plus</span></i><span> new fuel pipelines. Capture is simpler and has the same effect of making transport carbon neutral.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Even if it did, it’s hard for e-fuels to beat fossil fuels on price. In </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/i/166296864/what-green-hydrogen-needs"><span>this section</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref527ctzs57id"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn527ctzs57id">[2]</a></sup></span><span> I made some pretty favorable assumptions for green hydrogen and it wasn’t a slam dunk compared to fossil fuels. And DAC CO2 at $100/tonne is more expensive per mole carbon than fossil fuels.</span></p><p><span>Even assuming $1/kg hydrogen and $100/tonne CO2, natural gas comes out to $1.03/kg, roughly 6x higher than today’s prices. Further in the future, exhaustible resource economics will bite and the fossil price will pass the e-fuel price, but that takes a while.</span></p><p><span>So methane prices are about as low as they’ll ever be</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8552g6gcmj3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8552g6gcmj3">[3]</a></sup></span><span>. There will be price fluctuations, and better tech will help, but eventually we run out of breakthroughs. The economics of exhaustible resources demand that prices rise over the long term.</span></p><p><span>Instead of fuel, this tech would be valuable for making green ammonia, doing carbon capture, or making synthetic food. See my series on </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/p/net-zero-part-1-energy"><span>solving climate change</span></a><span> for more.</span></p><p><span>E-fuels aren’t going to save us, but most methane users can switch to alternatives. One application can’t go without it: rockets.</span></p><h1><span>Launch costs below $100/kg to LEO</span></h1><p><span>So e-fuels won’t save us money and methane prices won’t be lower than today. Now we have a lower bound on fuel costs for chemical rockets (which predominantly use methane and liquid oxygen).</span></p><p><span>Fuel is just one component of rocket costs, there’s engines and staff among other things. It’s hard to estimate prices for these, particularly as technology improves and manufacturing gets automated. That’s where the idiot index comes in.</span></p><p><span>Treating fuel as the “raw material” that goes into rockets, how many times larger should the final product (launch) be?</span></p><p><span>Today, fuel for a Starship launch is $1-2 million or 1% of overall costs; an idiot index of 100. SpaceX’s stated goal is to bring total launch cost down to $10 million, an idiot index of 10.</span></p><p><span>Looking at the idiot index for metals, they sit around 5. For passenger air travel, fuel costs are 15-25% of overall costs, an idiot index of 4-6. Launching and reusing rockets is more complicated than either of these, so an idiot index of 10 is generous.</span></p><p><span>Now to fuel prices, starting with methane. Looking at historical </span><a href="https://www.eia.gov/dnav/ng/hist/rngwhhdm.htm"><span>Henry Hub natural gas price</span></a><span> let’s assume $3/MCF. With 19.26 kg of natural gas per MCF, that’s $0.158/kg.</span></p><p><span>For liquid oxygen, I get conflicting estimates. Wikipedia has a lovely (but outdated) page on </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prices_of_chemical_elements"><span>Prices of chemical elements</span></a><span> claiming that oxygen is $0.154/kg. Let’s be generous and round that down to $0.1/kg, lower than most estimates</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref88g3mgsx13k"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn88g3mgsx13k">[4]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Using propellant masses </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship"><span>here</span></a><span>, we can estimate fuel costs for a Starship launch.</span></p><p><i><span>Booster fuel cost:</span></i></p><p><span>($0.158/kg * 700 tonnes methane + $0.1/kg * 2700 tonnes LOX )/ 100 tonnes payload = $3.81/kg</span></p><p><i><span>Starship fuel cost:</span></i></p><p><span>($0.158/kg * 330 tonnes methane + $0.1/kg * 1170 tonnes LOX )/ 100 tonnes payload = $1.69/kg</span></p><p><span>Totaling $5.5/kg in fuel costs. Applying a 10x idiot index, the overall cost is $55/kg. This is the most optimistic case I can make for chemical launch costs.</span></p><p><span>The fuel price will be higher than calculated here. For instance, natural gas consumers pay over </span><a href="https://www.eia.gov/dnav/ng/ng_pri_sum_a_EPG0_PCS_DMcf_a.htm"><span>$10/MCF</span></a><span> because of markups and delivery infrastructure. Rockets need high-purity liquid methane, which is probably why Starship fuel costs are 2-4x higher than my estimate. I doubt SpaceX</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreft4x7do113ng"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnt4x7do113ng">[5]</a></sup></span><span> is leaving money on the table, this is the best price they can get.</span></p><p><span>SpaceX Falcon Heavy costs closer to $2000/kg despite booster reuse, lots of flight experience, and a payload of 50 tonnes. Costs are higher than the theoretical minimum because this is literally rocket science!</span></p><p><span>$100/kg is probably the lowest cost we can hope for</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8fasi7i1ju5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8fasi7i1ju5">[6]</a></sup></span><span>. It would still be a revolution in launch, but if we want to go further, we need to start thinking about </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/p/the-economics-of-space-tethers"><span>space tethers</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/p/should-we-get-material-from-the-moon"><span>sourcing lunar material</span></a><span>.</span></p><h1><span>Asteroid mining</span></h1><p><span>Don’t take my word for it, Casey Handmer says </span><a href="https://caseyhandmer.wordpress.com/2019/08/27/there-are-no-known-commodity-resources-in-space-that-could-be-sold-on-earth/"><span>There are no known commodity resources in space that could be sold on Earth</span></a><span>. The moon in particular is made of the same crust as Earth, but with fewer geological processes to form veins of ore. There’s little reason to fly there and back.</span></p><p><span>Perhaps there are precious metals in the asteroid belt? Generally, Earth has more concentrated pockets of metal due to ore-forming processes, but asteroids do have higher concentrations of platinum group metals.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tgHpawHG4NbGhfghv/kbk5bynerrmoltch6r2d" /><p><a href="https://www.mining.com/wp-content/uploads/2023/04/Precious-and-structural-metals-on-asteroids-.pdf"><span>Source</span></a></p><p><span>One problem: by the time someone grabs an asteroid, extracts the precious metals, and lands on Earth, the demand for these metals may have collapsed. Consider </span><a href="https://www.thermofisher.com/blog/metals/platinum-group-metal-recovery-from-spent-catalytic-converters-using-xrf/"><span>that</span></a><span> “[a]bout 32% of the total Pt, 85% of the total Pd, and 90% of the total Rh were consumed by the automotive catalyst industry”. If we switch to electric cars during the decade they’re mining, the venture may be in the red</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftkjopdzelga"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntkjopdzelga">[7]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>The broader problem is that asteroid mining might cost more than the price of the materials being collected. Challenges include:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>A large delta V to most asteroids, with the “closest” requiring about </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0032063311001619"><span>4.5 km/s</span></a><span> of delta V from LEO. Compare to 5.7 km/s for the Moon.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Large financing costs since you need to buy expensive equipment and wait a long time for it to make the round trip. If it doesn’t break.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Poor solar resources and little fissile material in asteroids, making energy a big challenge.</span></li></ul><p><span>So maybe mining asteroids for precious metals isn’t the best idea. What about using asteroids for structural material in space? Well, the Moon is 30x larger than the entire asteroid belt, faster to get to, more amenable to space tethers, and awash in energy resources</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefp3yfk19e25"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnp3yfk19e25">[8]</a></sup></span><span>. So asteroids aren’t great for this.</span></p><p><span>There’s little reason to go to the asteroid belt</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8yuxjd132e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8yuxjd132e">[9]</a></sup></span><span>. Establishing the capability to fly to the belt and fling rocks at Earth is also not great. I think people focused on asteroid mining should </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/p/should-we-get-material-from-the-moon"><span>mine the moon</span></a><span> instead.</span></p><p><span>The broader lesson is that there’s no reason to bring anything back to Earth from space. Leave the mass up there where it’s most valuable.</span></p><h1><span>Space data centers</span></h1><p><span>Let me be clear about one thing: space data centers are </span><i><span>entirely</span></i><span> feasible from a technological perspective. Starlink already has solar, compute, cooling, interconnect, communications, etc. Nothing stops you from putting computers in space. It’s also entirely possible to make a profit doing computing in space.</span></p><p><span>But space data centers are not cost-competitive with terrestrial data centers.</span></p><p><span>People are comparing space data centers </span><i><span>on paper</span></i><span> to the terrestrial data centers of today. That’s not a fair comparison. You need to look at where progress in terrestrial data centers is going and correct for the optimism inherent to an on-paper assessment.</span></p><p><span>My argument examines the different parts of a datacenter (chips, interconnect, comms, cooling, energy, etc.) and shows that in space the cost per unit of performance is worse at every step. If every step is more expensive in space, then terrestrial data centers must be cheaper.</span></p><p><span>Even if you don’t buy my argument, I have some useful things to say about the design of such datacenters.</span></p><h3><span>Chips</span></h3><p><span>This point is relatively uncontested: computer hardware has a lower price-performance in space.</span></p><p><span>Chip performance is worse in space because:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Radiation causes bit flips and damages chips.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Space debris can destroy your data center</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefy1vaf0qoa98"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fny1vaf0qoa98">[10]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Maintenance is more infrequent, meaning more downtime and out-of-date hardware relative to terrestrial.</span></li></ul><p><span>Chip costs are higher in space because:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Chips need to be redesigned for launch and the space environment, including radiation shielding.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Maintenance is more expensive.</span></li><li value="3"><span>You need to pay launch costs</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref36r0h4khes3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn36r0h4khes3">[11]</a></sup></span><span> to get the hardware into space.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Additional costs of stationkeeping</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvdrywxlzxrq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvdrywxlzxrq">[12]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></li></ul><p><span>The size of these effects varies widely. For example, chips are very small, the cost to launch them is minuscule. </span><i><span>BUT</span></i><span> it’s a non-zero additional cost you pay to put chips in space. This necessarily makes it (slightly) more expensive to do computing in space relative to Earth.</span></p><p><span>These factors lower the price-performance of computing in space relative to Earth. They apply equally well to other parts of the server such as interconnects, racks, wiring, and so on.</span></p><p><span>While servers and networking are </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/data-insights/ai-datacenter-cost-breakdown"><span>73%</span></a><span> of data center costs, they’re not the only cost. If other areas become much cheaper in space, then the cost of putting chips in space might be worthwhile.</span></p><h3><span>Cooling</span></h3><p><span>Chips need to be near each other to keep interconnect latency low. These chips take energy from solar panels and produce heat. That heat needs to be dissipated into a radiator.</span></p><p><span>Rather than look at terrestrial data center cooling costs and speculate about the design of future satellite radiators, consider a simple test:</span></p><p><b><span>Imagine we design a radiator for space. We make two copies: one goes to space and the other stays on the ground. Which rejects more heat?</span></b></p><p><span>This is a </span><i><span>very</span></i><span> rough test; but it works for our purposes. If the space radiator has better performance on Earth, we can safely claim that space datacenters have higher cost-per-unit-cooling.</span></p><p><span>Why? One, because launching the radiator into space adds additional cost and lead time. Two, because a radiator designed for space makes the terrestrial performance look worse. We don’t use space radiators on Earth because we have better options.</span></p><p><span>In other words, this is an optimistic case for space radiators, enabling us to rule them out if they perform worse in space.</span></p><p><span>For both space and Earth, radiators enjoy radiative heat transfer, governed by the Stefan-Boltzmann Law:</span></p><p><span>Where </span><i><span>ε</span></i><span> is the emissivity of the radiator material, </span><i><span>σ</span></i><span> is a constant, </span><i><span>T</span></i><span> is the temperature of the radiator, and </span><i><span>T_env</span></i><span> is the temperature of the environment.</span></p><p><span>Earth also enjoys convective heat transfer governed by Newton’s law of cooling:</span></p><p><span>Where </span><i><span>h</span></i><span> is the convective heat transfer coefficient and </span><i><span>A</span></i><span> is the area of the radiator.</span></p><p><span>Some assumptions:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>In both cases, the radiators are shielded from the sun’s radiation by the solar panels. The space radiator is also perfectly shielded from the Earth’s radiation. In practice the latter is not feasible because the Earth takes up a large part of the satellite’s horizon, but this is an optimistic case for the satellite.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Environmental temperature on Earth is 293 K, environmental temperature in space is 2.7 K (CMB)</span></li><li value="3"><span>The radiator is 1 m² of anodized aluminum with an emissivity of 0.9. Emissivity can’t be greater than 1, so there isn’t much room for better materials here.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Choosing the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_transfer_coefficient"><span>heat transfer coefficient</span></a><span> is tricky. After looking at some examples, 10 W/m2-K seems reasonable</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefschdwotpcoc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnschdwotpcoc">[13]</a></sup></span><span>. Adding a fan would 10x this number but that messes up the cost comparison.</span></li></ol><p><span>Now we can look at the heat radiated as we change the temperature of the radiator (different from the temperature of the chips).</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tgHpawHG4NbGhfghv/xcurmvliby4piop14ls4" /><p><span>Code </span><a href="https://colab.research.google.com/drive/1Uml2XQ1F4v-NAL7UiWriaOTD_gH5ikYJ?usp=sharing"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Terrestrial chips operate around ~75°C. At this temperature, the radiator on earth rejects more heat than the one in space, and the gap grows wider at higher temperatures</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefz2xqusx01t"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnz2xqusx01t">[14]</a></sup></span><span>. Adding a fan to the Earth radiator (h=100) extends its advantage down to around 25°C.</span></p><p><b><span>For a wide range of radiator temperatures, the space radiator works better on Earth than in space. Given the additional costs of sending the radiator to space, cooling is cheaper on Earth than in space.</span></b></p><p><span>I’m glossing over details here, like the different temperatures for the chips and the working fluid; optimizations around chip temperature, leakage current, and compressor energy consumption. It’s plausible that we invent chips that make space radiators look better (see discussion of reversible computers). Then again, it’s plausible we invent chips that make the </span><i><span>Earth</span></i><span> radiator look better.</span></p><p><span>For now, it’s safe to say that cooling costs will be higher in space than on Earth.</span></p><p><span>The Earth’s atmosphere is a giant radiator. It emits 174 petawatts into space, seven orders of magnitude more than all AI capacity today</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6pg7d7y65re"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6pg7d7y65re">[15]</a></sup></span><span>. We’re nowhere close to saturating Earth’s compute capacity. This is why space AI is </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/i/176931668/will-anyone-want-space-compute"><span>only interesting</span></a><span> if demand outpaces Earth’s capacity to provide it.</span></p><p><span>See also:</span></p><p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FlQYU3m1e80"><span>Is It Really Impossible To Cool A Datacenter In Space? - YouTube</span></a></p><p><span>A humorous but information dense </span><a href="https://x.com/andrewmccalip/status/2010185417799115120?s=20"><span>thread</span></a><span> from Andrew McCalip.</span></p><h3><span>Energy</span></h3><p><span>Energy is </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/data-insights/ai-datacenter-cost-breakdown"><span>~7%</span></a><span> of overall costs, so even if energy was free in space, costs would be at most 7% lower</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbrt1ncebzkn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbrt1ncebzkn">[16]</a></sup></span><span>. Given that hardware costs 10x more and should increase in cost in space, that already looks like a bad trade.</span></p><p><span>There are three challenges for solar in space. The first was covered in the hardware section; hardware generally has a lower cost per unit performance in space including radiation, debris, maintenance costs, and launch costs.</span></p><p><span>The second is heat. Panels must face the sun and cool radiatively, leading to high equilibrium temperatures. Panels on the ISS reach </span><a href="https://space.stackexchange.com/questions/14256/what-is-the-temperature-of-solar-panels-used-in-space-missions-such-as-iss"><span>70°C in full sunlight</span></a><span>, which hurts efficiency. This means using efficient and expensive panels (since any light not converted to electricity becomes heat, higher efficiency lowers equilibrium temperature).</span></p><p><span>The third challenge is stiffness. Panels need to be stowed in the rocket in such a way that they don’t get shaken apart by the ~40 Hz vibration during launch. Avoiding this requires stiffness which adds mass and cost. Once deployed, there are limits to how thin the panels can get. Varying gravitational forces can induce </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0094576520306378"><span>thin panels to flop</span></a><span> and moving parts on the satellite can drive vibrational modes.</span></p><p><span>Now for a calculation:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>The panel is in dawn-dusk SSO with constant illumination at 1362 W/m2.</span></li><li value="2"><span>A 5 year lifespan before the computer hardware has to be swapped out (with the upfront cost of swapping out the chips and the risk of panel failure, it will likely be more economical to replace the panels at the same time).</span></li><li value="3"><span>The GaAs panels are 30% efficient at 70°C</span></li><li value="4"><span>A panel mass per meter squared of 3 kg/m2. This is slightly heavier than the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrical_system_of_the_International_Space_Station#Solar_array_wing"><span>panels on the ISS</span></a><span>, but accounts for additional structural mass required in the fairing and during deployment.</span></li></ol><p><span>Result: </span><a href="https://www.wolframalpha.com/input?i=%283+kg%2Fm%5E2+*+%24100%2Fkg%29%2F%280.30*1362+W%2Fm%5E2+*+5+years%29+to+%24%2FMWh"><span>$16.76/MWh</span></a><span> before accounting for the idiot index. What index should we use to account for panel costs, design, assembly, and testing? Surely a factor of 3 is generous? The resulting $50/MWh is comparable to terrestrial solar today, but solar costs will continue to fall (see section on solar costs).</span></p><p><span>Space solar will continue to improve. More efficient and thinner cells may be achievable. Instead of areal density, let’s look at specific power. </span><a href="https://www.nasa.gov/smallsat-institute/sst-soa/power-subsystems/"><span>This page</span></a><span> has a large list of different space panels and their specific power. It seems like 200 W/kg is an optimistic goal. A 5-year lifetime gives us $34/MWh with a 3x idiot index. Once again, probably not better than terrestrial solar.</span></p><p><span>It’s possible that new designs will improve the specific power enough to outpace advances in terrestrial solar, but once again, cutting down the small fraction of energy costs while damaging the hardware does not look promising.</span></p><h3><span>Communications</span></h3><p><span>Satellites are good for long distance communications. Light travels faster in a vacuum than in optical fiber, and satellites enjoy long lines of sight. So if you’re trying to communicate with someone far away, satellite internet works well.</span></p><p><span>But for AI inference, it’s easier to park compute close to users on Earth than in space. This is particularly true for tasks that demand extremely low latency. Robots, drones, and autonomous vehicles will need compute onboard, excluding space AI from these lucrative use cases</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftwhg4e2ez9e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntwhg4e2ez9e">[17]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>It’s a closer race for generative AI running in distant data centers. But space AI still struggles to compete. While Starlink latency is comparable to cellular data and only slightly slower than fiber internet, it has lower bandwidth and higher cost for most users. These facts are unlikely to change, Starlink is not planning to beat traditional internet on cost or bandwidth.</span></p><p><span>AI in particular adds new latency challenges. Satellites in LEO stay visible by your antenna for ~5 minutes. They spend some amount of time flying over oceans and sparsely populated areas. To avoid low utilization, queries need to be dynamically routed to different satellites. This problem is solvable, but the routing means that responses take longer to reach users relative to terrestrial.</span></p><p><span>So once again, the cost performance is worse than terrestrial counterparts. This is particularly true if we start parking data centers in cities and running more fiber.</span></p><h3><span>Permitting</span></h3><p><span>A common argument for space compute is that the permitting challenges are easier. I have yet to see an argument for why permitting is somehow easier when building launch pads, firing off hundreds of rockets, and filling up limited orbital slots. Rocket launches have permitting too! Indeed, there is more bureaucracy in launch because you have to deal with local ordinances, aviation restrictions, communications law, and international law</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffusigffy0f4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfusigffy0f4">[18]</a></sup></span><span>. As we learn more about </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w677o1jHYmk"><span>launch pollution</span></a><span>, restrictions may tighten further.</span></p><p><span>I’ll edit in an additional response here if I see a good argument for why permitting will be easier for rockets than laying down solar panels.</span></p><h3><span>A better way</span></h3><p><span>This criticism lays the groundwork for a better approach to space compute.</span></p><p><span>For example, people are talking about increasing the chip temperature in space to decrease the size of the radiator. This is silly because increasing chip temperature increases things like leakage current, thus </span><i><span>raising</span></i><span> energy cost per FLOP and counteracting the benefits of a smaller radiator</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefohnajijvjvs"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnohnajijvjvs">[19]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Go the other way! As we saw in the cooling section, space radiators look better at low temperatures. Launch costs are proportional to mass, and the mass of the hardware and radiators is small relative to the panels. You can lower the energy cost per FLOP if you cool the chips or do reversible computing. The panels can shrink while the radiator grows more slowly</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqqrjsd3zqhe"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqqrjsd3zqhe">[20]</a></sup></span><span>, lowering mass in net.</span></p><p><span>Reversible computers create an advantage to space over Earth. Space is cold, it’s easier to keep your chips cold in space than on Earth. That’s why reversible computing featured prominently in my post on the </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/p/semiconductors-will-see-an-end-of"><span>end of semiconductors</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>What should space compute be used for? Onboard processing of satellite imagery, military intelligence, telecommunications, and </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/i/136435878/communications"><span>stock markets in space</span></a><span> are all far more profitable opportunities than AI inference. See that linked post for other ideas.</span></p><p><span>If you insist on AI inference in space, </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/p/an-intro-to-the-tensor-economics"><span>the economics</span></a><span> push for large numbers of chips linked together with lots of fiber (perhaps spread out on the back of the solar panels). A single massive datacenter in orbit with satellite internet gathering requests from across the globe makes the most sense</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkjxkdxvlxh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkjxkdxvlxh">[21]</a></sup></span><span>. The latency of this compute means it’s best for the </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/p/futures-for-llm-inference"><span>low speed, low cost niche</span></a><span> suited to autonomous AI workers.</span></p><h3><span>So … space AI?</span></h3><p><span>To be clear, it’s entirely possible to compute in space. It’s also entirely possible to make a profit computing in space. But it probably won’t be cost competitive with terrestrial datacenters. Every part of the tech stack has a lower performance in space relative to terrestrial.</span></p><p><span>You could fight this conclusion by stacking </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/p/the-economics-of-space-tethers"><span>tethers</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/p/should-we-get-material-from-the-moon"><span>lunar materials</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/p/semiconductors-will-see-an-end-of"><span>reversible compute</span></a><span>, but then we’re not making a fair comparison to terrestrial compute. Terrestrial compute has a lot of opportunities to improve</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnb0csk4l1ej"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnb0csk4l1ej">[22]</a></sup></span><span>. By the time we build space infrastructure, we might be synthesizing solar from dirt or using a laptop AI to do inference over a </span><a href="https://bsky.app/profile/harsimony.bsky.social/post/3mk4vpar4rs23"><span>hypercompressed local internet</span></a><span>.</span></p><h3><span>Space doesn’t need to be profitable</span></h3><p><span>My fellow space enthusiasts, your dreams don’t have to be profitable to be worthwhile. For most endeavors, AI will teach us that anyways.</span></p><p><span>Going to space provides little economic benefit for those who are content to stay on Earth</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdc66wcc9pf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndc66wcc9pf">[23]</a></sup></span><span>. It’s </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/i/176931668/will-anyone-want-space-compute"><span>not clear</span></a><span> that even the promise of a Dyson sphere could coax our economy into space</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrx8arswq0ch"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrx8arswq0ch">[24]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Instead, we will go to space out of desire, not necessity. Because we enjoy such widespread abundance on Earth that anyone can reach the heavens.</span></p><p><b><span>Further reading on space data centers, with an emphasis on good technical arguments and actually doing math.</span></b></p><p><span>Skeptics:</span></p><p><a href="https://andrewmccalip.com/space-datacenters"><span>Economics of Orbital vs Terrestrial Data Centers</span></a></p><p><a href="https://andercot.substack.com/p/do-orbital-data-centers-make-sense"><span>Do Orbital Data Centers Make Sense? - by Andrew Cote</span></a></p><p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mA-S1JGzph4"><span>The Truth About SpaceX’s “Orbital Datacenters” - YouTube</span></a></p><p><a href="https://www.dwarkesh.com/p/notes-on-space-gpus"><span>Notes on Space GPUs - by Dwarkesh Patel</span></a></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/65ECgHzWxTRvt8XWK/will-we-really-put-data-centers-in-space"><span>Will we really put data centers in space?</span></a></p><p><span>Casey Handmer is more neutral here, though he thinks space AI would be 2x more expensive than terrestrial:</span></p><p><span>His </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/u/0/d/1JoLLcmTBXpWv7KRtwUsfYeOAyqjk21jPvdz-ndKV5WM/htmlview"><span>Orbital inference</span></a><span> Spreadsheet.</span></p><p><a href="https://caseyhandmer.wordpress.com/2026/01/30/direct-current-data-centers/"><span>Direct Current Data Centers – Casey Handmer’s blog</span></a></p><p><a href="https://caseyhandmer.wordpress.com/2026/02/10/i-guess-were-doing-moon-factories-now/"><span>Space AI: I guess we’re doing Moon factories now – Casey Handmer’s blog</span></a></p><p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=vkhZHR_hs4c"><span>SpaceX’s AI Data Centres Might Actually Be A Good Idea. Here’s Why - YouTube</span></a></p><p><span>Optimists:</span></p><p><a href="https://research.33fg.com/analysis/the-solar-power-unlock-for-spacex-s-100-kw-ton-compute-satellites"><span>Space Intelligence</span></a></p><p><a href="https://unchartedterritories.tomaspueyo.com/p/can-we-build-ai-in-space"><span>Can We Build AI in Space?</span></a></p><p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cLcF9UCD9-s"><span>Why Space-Based AI Data Centers Are Inevitable: 3 Levels of Analysis</span></a></p><h1><span>Ramjets and demand for Mach 3+ flight</span></h1><p><span>After my post on </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/p/rocketplanes-update"><span>rocketplanes</span></a><span>, I looked at hypersonic flight times between major cities. Mach 3 can get you between most major cities in under 3 hours.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tgHpawHG4NbGhfghv/gauptbttcmwsfkglzet6" /><p><br /></p><p><span>Mach 3 is achievable with existing tech. The SR-71 blackbird used a turbofan with afterburner for an official top speed of Mach 3.3 (the real number is likely higher). In this regime, turbofans are more efficient than advanced concepts like ramjets.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tgHpawHG4NbGhfghv/ehob4bpt5yvva51a86gx" /><p><span>So Mach 3 is fast, fuel-efficient, and uses existing tech. Is it worth the headache of going Mach 5 if you only shave an hour or so off your flight time? It’s not even clear the time savings will materialize if a Mach 5 plane needs more pre-flight checks or has to stop more frequently to refuel.</span></p><p><span>I’m still excited about companies like </span><a href="https://www.astromecha.co/"><span>AstroMechanica</span></a><span>, it’s great to see people try new things here. But after the dust settles, the future of passenger air travel looks to be in Mach 3, </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/p/turning-airplanes-into-air-busses"><span>improving traditional flight</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/i/176707738/3"><span>flying cars</span></a><span>.</span></p><h1><span>Passenger rail</span></h1><p><span>Trains are great for moving stuff over land, but moving people? Nope.</span></p><p><span>As Eli Dourado says, </span><a href="https://x.com/elidourado/status/1964543047209009179"><span>trains are not an abundance technology</span></a><span>. If you want to go long distances quickly, an airplane can get you there faster and cheaper. And without the headache of building massive amounts of track with low utilization.</span></p><p><span>What about public transit in dense urban areas? Well, </span><a href="https://ideasindevelopment.substack.com/i/197318851/bus-good-train-bad"><span>Ed Glaeser says</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Forty years of transportation economics at Harvard can be boiled down to four words. Bus good, train bad.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Simply maintaining roads, optimizing bus stops, and using </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/i/152527591/4"><span>congestion pricing to maximize throughput</span></a><span> will produce a cost effective transit system. Ride-sharing </span><a href="https://splittinginfinity.substack.com/p/new-cities-for-self-driving-cars"><span>autonomous vehicles</span></a><span> and busses are about to make the case for roads even stronger.</span></p><h1><span>Fusion</span></h1><p><span>Fusion generates energy by colliding two atomic nuclei and releasing energy in the form of neutrons, electromagnetic radiation, and charged particles. The radiation can be converted to energy in two ways. One, charged particles can generate energy via </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_energy_conversion"><span>direct energy conversion</span></a><span>. Or two, X-rays, gamma-rays, and neutrons can be absorbed by radiation shielding, producing heat</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnrhnycqdja"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnrhnycqdja">[25]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>We can probably rule out the second method. Fission reactors already do this using simpler, de-risked technology. They’ve been unable to compete with other forms of energy generation</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbhz3743ym4o"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbhz3743ym4o">[26]</a></sup></span><span>. Using fusion to produce even-more-expensive neutrons is not promising.</span></p><p><span>Direct energy conversion is interesting. Avoiding steam turbines is a big reason why solar is cheap. Maybe fusion could be cheap too.</span></p><p><span>I’m going to focus on D-D fusion for our optimistic analysis. While few companies are pursuing this (it’s harder than D-T fusion), it looks better for producing charged particles and the cheaper fuel lowers the on-paper cost. Assumptions:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>The reactor performs Deuterium-Deuterium fusion without fuel reprocessing. When including side reactions with Tritium, this produces 8.35 MeV from 5 Deuterium atoms. That is </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_fusion#Neutronicity,_confinement_requirement,_and_power_density"><span>1.67 MeV</span></a><span> of charged particles per Deuterium atom. Much of this energy comes from side-reactions with Tritium. This is the largest amount of energy a D-D reactor could produce from charged particles alone.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Charged particles can be converted into electricity at a </span><a href="https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.13182/FST83-A20820"><span>50% efficiency</span></a><span> (probably higher than achievable at scale). Each deuterium atom produces 0.835 MeV.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Deuterium costs </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prices_of_chemical_elements"><span>$4K/kg</span></a><span>. This is highly uncertain, I’m basing it off of an out-of-date Wikipedia page. See the footnote</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbxf4ayafwaf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbxf4ayafwaf">[27]</a></sup></span><span> for more.</span></li><li value="4"><span>An idiot index of 100. Also tricky to estimate, but the idiot index for fission plants is 10 and fusion promises to use much fancier equipment and more technical staff.</span></li><li value="5"><span>We ignore the value of energy produced via neutrons from the D-T side reaction.
